Maybe we can rewrite the query to use `EXISTS` for the date_dim d3 condition to avoid joining d3? The condition `d3.d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day'` where d3.d_date_sk = cs_ship_date_sk. We can replace the join to d3 with a filter on cs_ship_date_sk using a subquery on date_dim. For example:
```
AND cs_ship_date_sk IN (SELECT d_date_sk FROM date_dim WHERE d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day')
```
But this subquery depends on d1.d_date, so cannot be precomputed. It would be a correlated subquery. The current join to d3 is using index scan on date_dim d3 for each row, which is cheap. Not a bottleneck.

=== Worker 0: Start Rewrite ===
 index. However, query rewriting alone might not reduce cost. The most efficient way is to remove unused left joins (if not already optimized) and perhaps add index. But since explain already eliminated them, the SQL is already optimized by the DBMS. Yet the query text has unnecessary joins. The mission is to perform high-quality query rewriting. So we should rewrite to remove the left joins. Also we can add explicit inner joins and format. That's a rewrite. We can also mention that adding a composite index on `catalog_sales(cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost)` would be the biggest performance win, but that's not a SQL rewrite. The final SQL will be the rewritten query without left joins.

Let's consider if removing left joins changes anything if catalog_returns has multiple matches. We argued no for MIN. But wait: The original `LEFT OUTER JOIN catalog_returns` can multiply rows. If a catalog_sales row matches multiple catalog_returns, it appears multiple times. ```sql
SELECT MIN(i.i_item_sk),
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name),
       MIN(d1.d_week_seq),
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk),
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number),
       MIN(inv.inv_item_sk)
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
WHERE d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq
  AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2000
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'S'
  AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 6 AND 8
  AND i.i_category IN ('Children', 'Men', 'Women')
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 69 AND 89;
```

# EXPLAIN Comparison Report

## 1. Cost Efficiency

- **Overall cost change percentage:** 0%. The rewritten plan and the enhanced plan both report exactly the same total cost as the original plan (131,898.99 → 131,899.00). No rewrite or enhancement changed the optimizer's cost model output.
- **Cost change at the most expensive plan node:** 0%. The dominant node in all three plans is the inner **Nested Loop (cost=128,560.13)** feeding the Parallel Hash, itself dominated by the **Hash Join (cost=127,108.41)**. Its cost is identical in all three plans.

## 2. Plan Characteristics

- **Scan type transitions:** None. All three plans retain:
  - Parallel Seq Scan on `date_dim d2`
  - Parallel Index Scan on `date_dim d1` (via `idx_date_dim_sk`)
  - Index Scans on `catalog_sales` (sold-date index), `customer_demographics`, `item`, `inventory` (pkey), `warehouse` (pkey), and `date_dim d3`
  - Seq Scan on `household_demographics`
  No Seq Scan was converted to an Index Scan, and no Index Scan was downgraded or upgraded.
- **Join algorithm refinements:** None. The join hierarchy is unchanged: Nested Loops at the outer levels, one Parallel Hash Join (`d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq`), and one inner Hash Join on `cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd_demo_sk`. No Hash→Merge or other join-type refinement occurred.
- **Elimination of explicit sorting / reduction of intermediate result sets:** No explicit Sort nodes existed in the original plan (the aggregate is handled via Finalize/Partial Aggregate over the Gather), and none were added or removed. Row estimates and widths at every node are unchanged, so intermediate result set sizes are identical.

## 3. Resource Utilization

- **Memory usage (Hash/Buffer nodes):** Unchanged. The Parallel Hash build node (cost=128,560.13) and the inner Hash node over `household_demographics` (cost=143.00, ~1,200 rows) are identical across all three plans; estimated hash-table memory consumption is therefore the same.
- **Worker count adjustment:** Unchanged. All three plans use **Workers Planned: 1** under the Gather node (i.e., 1 worker + the leader = 2 parallel participants). No change in parallelism was applied.

## 4. Other Improvements

- **Readability improvement only:** The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce explicit short aliases (`cs`, `hd`, `cd`, `i`, `inv`, `w`, `d1/d2/d3`) in place of full table names. This reduces query text verbosity and display width but has **no effect** on the execution plan, costs, or performance.
- **Semantic equivalence confirmed:** Because every cost, row, and operator matches, the rewrite is verified to be a plan-preserving transformation — it neither helped nor harmed performance.
- **Residual bottleneck for future tuning:** The plan remains dominated by the deeply nested loop chain (cost ≈ 128.5k, out of a total of ≈ 131.9k, i.e., >97% of plan cost), particularly the outer Nested Loop probing `catalog_sales` per d1 row and the interval-based `d3.d_date > d1.d_date + '3 days'` join filter evaluated above the aggregate input. Future optimization efforts (e.g., materializing the d1/cs candidate set, partitioning on `cs_sold_date_sk`, or rewriting the temporal filter) should target this Nested Loop chain rather than the alias/structure changes attempted here.

**Conclusion:** The rewritten and enhanced SQL produce plans identical to the original — 0% cost change overall and at the hottest node, no scan/join algorithm transitions, no sorting changes, and identical memory and worker usage. The only observable difference is cosmetic (aliasing), with the true cost hotspots left untouched.
</analysis>
A line-by-line comparison of the three EXPLAIN outputs shows that all three plans are structurally and numerically identical: every cost boundary (start-up cost, total cost), row estimate, width, scan method, join method, and worker count matches exactly across the original, rewritten, and enhanced plans. The only textual difference is that the rewritten and enhanced versions introduce explicit table aliases (cs, hd, cd, i, inv, w, d1–d3) instead of full table names, which shortens the display width (e.g., catalog_sales width drops from 32 to 28 in the display, purely a formatting artifact of aliasing, not a change in the projected columns). Since no plan-shape transformation occurred, the cost analysis concludes with a 0% change at both the overall plan level and the most expensive node. The dominant cost driver in all three plans remains the deep Nested Loop chain feeding the Parallel Hash Join at cost=128,560.16, and within that, the Hash Join node at 127,108.41 built on a Parallel Index Scan + Index Scan probe over catalog_sales. Therefore the report focuses on confirming plan stability and identifying the residual bottleneck (the ~127k-cost Nested Loop with the interval-based join filter) as the target for any future optimization, rather than reporting improvements.
</analysis>

</report>
# EXPLAIN Comparison Report

## 1. Cost Efficiency

- **Overall cost change percentage:** 0%. The rewritten plan and the enhanced plan both report exactly the same total cost as the original plan (131,898.99 → 131,899.00). No rewrite or enhancement changed the optimizer's cost model output.
- **Cost change at the most expensive plan node:** 0%. The dominant node in all three plans is the inner **Nested Loop (cost=128,560.13)** feeding the Parallel Hash, itself dominated by the **Hash Join (cost=127,108.41)**. Its cost is identical in all three plans.

## 2. Plan Characteristics

- **Scan type transitions:** None. All three plans retain:
  - Parallel Seq Scan on `date_dim d2`
  - Parallel Index Scan on `date_dim d1` (via `idx_date_dim_sk`)
  - Index Scans on `catalog_sales` (sold-date index), `customer_demographics`, `item`, `inventory` (pkey), `warehouse` (pkey), and `date_dim d3`
  - Seq Scan on `household_demographics`
  No Seq Scan was converted to an Index Scan, and no Index Scan was downgraded or upgraded.
- **Join algorithm refinements:** None. The join hierarchy is unchanged: Nested Loops at the outer levels, one Parallel Hash Join (`d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq`), and one inner Hash Join on `cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd_demo_sk`. No Hash→Merge or other join-type refinement occurred.
- **Elimination of explicit sorting / reduction of intermediate result sets:** No explicit Sort nodes existed in the original plan (the aggregate is handled via Finalize/Partial Aggregate over the Gather), and none were added or removed. Row estimates and widths at every node are unchanged, so intermediate result set sizes are identical.

## 3. Resource Utilization

- **Memory usage (Hash/Buffer nodes):** Unchanged. The Parallel Hash build node (cost=128,560.13) and the inner Hash node over `household_demographics` (cost=143.00, ~1,200 rows) are identical across all three plans; estimated hash-table memory consumption is therefore the same.
- **Worker count adjustment:** Unchanged. All three plans use **Workers Planned: 1** under the Gather node (i.e., 1 worker + the leader = 2 parallel participants). No change in parallelism was applied.

## 4. Other Improvements

- **Readability improvement only:** The rewritten and enhanced plans introduce explicit short aliases (`cs`, `hd`, `cd`, `i`, `inv`, `w`, `d1/d2/d3`) in place of full table names. This reduces query text verbosity and display width but has **no effect** on the execution plan, costs, or performance.
- **Semantic equivalence confirmed:** Because every cost, row, and operator matches, the rewrite is verified to be a plan-preserving transformation — it neither helped nor harmed performance.
- **Residual bottleneck for future tuning:** The plan remains dominated by the deeply nested loop chain (cost ≈ 128.5k, out of a total of ≈ 131.9k, i.e., >97% of plan cost), particularly the outer Nested Loop probing `catalog_sales` per d1 row and the interval-based `d3.d_date > d1.d_date + '3 days'` join filter evaluated above the aggregate input. Future optimization efforts (e.g., materializing the d1/cs candidate set, partitioning on `cs_sold_date_sk`, or rewriting the temporal filter) should target this Nested Loop chain rather than the alias/structure changes attempted here.

**Conclusion:** The rewritten and enhanced SQL produce plans identical to the original — 0% cost change overall and at the hottest node, no scan/join algorithm transitions, no sorting changes, and identical memory and worker usage. The only observable difference is cosmetic (aliasing), with the true cost hotspots left untouched.

Let's craft result final. is a single row with all mins. If we compute them separately, we might do multiple scans. But we can put them in a single SELECT with scalar subqueries. The planner might execute them separately, but each could use a more efficient plan. Let's see:
- For min(cs_item_sk), min(cs_order_number), min(d1.d_week_seq): we need catalog_sales joined with date_dim d1, d3, item, cd, hd, and EXISTS inventory matching item, week, quantity. This still requires checking inventory for each cs row, but as an EXISTS semi-join. It might be more efficient because it stops at first matching inventory row and doesn't join warehouse. But the current plan also stops? The join to inventory might produce multiple rows, but since we aggregate MIN, duplicates don't matter. The planner doesn't know that, so it processes all matching inventory rows. Using EXISTS would allow early exit per cs row. That could be a significant optimization! Because the current join to inventory can produce multiple rows per catalog_sales row (multiple inventory rows in the week with quantity < cs_quantity). The aggregates then process all those rows. Since MIN is duplicate-insensitive, we only need to know if at least one matching inventory row exists for the catalog_sales row to include it in the mins of cs columns. For min(w_warehouse_name), we need the minimum warehouse name among all matching inventory rows for all cs rows. But we could compute that separately with a query that joins inventory and checks existence of cs. So we can split the query into two parts:
1. Compute min over catalog_sales rows that have at least one matching inventory row (for min cs_item_sk, cs_order_number, d1.d_week_seq, i_item_sk, inv_item_sk). Actually min(inv_item_sk) = min(cs_item_sk) for those rows. min(i_item_sk) = min(cs_item_sk). So we only need min(cs_item_sk), min(cs_order_number), min(d1.d_week_seq) over such cs rows.
2. Compute min(w_warehouse_name) over all matching (cs, inv) pairs. This requires joining cs and inv to get warehouse names, but we only need the minimum. We can compute it by scanning inventory/warehouse and checking EXISTS of matching cs row.

If we do this, we can avoid the join multiplicity for the first part. Let's formulate:

Part 1:
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(cs.cs_order_number), MIN(d1.d_week_seq)
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
WHERE d1.d_year = 2000
  AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'S'
  AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 6 AND 8
  AND i.i_category IN ('Children', 'Men', 'Women')
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 69 AND 89
  AND EXISTS (
    SELECT 1
    FROM inventory inv
    JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
    WHERE inv.inv_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
      AND d2.d_week_seq = d1.d_week_seq
      AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  );

Part 2:
SELECT MIN(w.w_warehouse_name)
FROM inventory

=== Worker 1: Start Rewrite ===

JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk

JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk

Let's check: In pre_rewrite_sql, the SELECT is:
SELECT MIN(i.i_item_sk), MIN(w.w_warehouse_name), MIN(d1.d_week_seq), MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(cs.cs_order_number), MIN(inv.inv_item_sk)
If we replace MIN(i.i_item_sk) with MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) and MIN(inv.inv_item_sk) with MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), we get:
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(w.w_warehouse_name), MIN(d1.d_week_seq), MIN(cs.cs_item_sk), MIN(cs.cs_order_number), MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)
This is equivalent because the joins enforce equality. This might allow the planner to not need the `i_item_sk` and `inv_item_sk` columns from the joins, potentially enabling join elimination? Wait, if we no longer select any column from `item` other than the filter, and we no longer select `inv_item_sk`, can we eliminate the `item` join? No, because we still have `i.i_category IN (...)`. But we could rewrite that filter as `cs.cs_item_sk IN (SELECT i_item_sk FROM item WHERE i_category IN (...))`. That would allow eliminating the join to item entirely, replacing it with a semi-join. Similarly, for inventory, we still need `inv_quantity_on_hand < cs_quantity` and `w_warehouse_name` and `inv_warehouse_sk`, so we can't eliminate inventory. But we can eliminate the need for `inv_item_sk` in the SELECT. The join condition `cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk` is still needed. So no join elimination there.

What about the `item` join? If we replace `MIN(i.i_item_sk)` with `MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)` and change the join to a semi-join via `EXISTS`, we can remove the `item` join. The filter `i.i_category IN ('Children','Men','Women')` becomes `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM item i WHERE i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk AND i.i_category IN (...))`. Since item has 102k rows, this semi-join might be efficient. But the current plan already does an index scan on item per cs row (only 9 rows), so it's not a bottleneck. However, removing the join might slightly reduce the plan cost and complexity. It also makes the query more explicit that item is only used for filtering. This is a legitimate query rewrite. Let's see the cost: The current plan has a nested loop with item index scan. If we use EXISTS, the planner might use a semi-join (hash or nested loop) and possibly filter earlier. It might not change the plan much, but it's a structural improvement.

Similarly, we can remove the `warehouse` join for the `MIN(w_warehouse_name)`? No, we need it. But we could compute `MIN(w_warehouse_name)` via a subquery? Not helpful.

What about the `date_dim d3` join? It's used only for `d3.d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day'`. We could rewrite this as a filter on `cs_ship_date_sk` using a subquery: `EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM date_dim d3 WHERE d3.d_date_sk = cs.cs_ship_date_sk AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day')`. But that's essentially the same join. Not helpful.

The `customer_demographics` and `household_demographics` joins are used for filters. They could be rewritten as EXISTS, but they are small tables and the planner handles them well.

Given the mission's emphasis on CTEs when complex WHERE/JOIN conditions or redundant subquery calculations, we could use a CTE to pre-filter catalog_sales with all the dimension filters, reducing the size before joining inventory. This is essentially the `cs_filtered` CTE I wrote. Even if the planner inlines it, it improves readability. We can present that as the optimized SQL. Let's craft a final query that:
- Uses CTEs for readability.
- Removes unused left joins (promotion, catalog_returns).
- Uses explicit INNER JOINs and aliases.
- Replaces `MIN(i_item_sk)` and `MIN(inv_item_sk)` with `MIN(cs_item_sk)` since they are equal.
- Uses a CTE to filter catalog_sales with all dimension filters (date, customer, household, item category) and the non-inventory predicates. This CTE will include cs_item_sk, cs_order_number, cs_quantity, d1.d_week_seq, d1.d_date, cs_sold_date_sk, cs_ship_date_sk, etc. Then join to inventory and warehouse, and aggregate. This might help the planner if it materializes the CTE, but even if not, it's cleaner.

Let's write it:

WITH filtered_cs AS (
    SELECT cs.cs_item_sk,
           cs.cs_order_number,
           cs.cs_quantity,
           d1.d_week_seq,
           d1.d_date,
           cs.cs_sold_date_sk,
           cs.cs_ship_date_sk
    FROM catalog_sales cs
    JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
    JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
    JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
    JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
    JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
    WHERE d1.d_year = 2000
      AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
      AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
      AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'S'
      AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 6 AND 8
      AND i.i_category IN ('Children', 'Men', 'Women')
      AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 69 AND 89
)
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_i_item_sk,
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_w_warehouse_name,
       MIN(cs.d_week_seq) AS min_d1_d_week_seq,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_cs_item_sk,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_cs_order_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_inv_item_sk
FROM filtered_cs cs
JOIN date_dim d2 ON d2.d_week_seq = cs.d_week_seq
JOIN inventory inv ON inv.inv_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
                  AND inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
                  AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk;

This is semantically equivalent? Let's check: Original had `join inventory on (cs_item_sk = inv_item_sk)` and `join date_dim d2 on (inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk)` and `where d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq`. In our query, we join `date_dim d2 ON d2.d_week_seq = cs.d_week_seq` and then join inventory on `inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk AND inv.inv_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk`. This is equivalent: for each cs row, we find d2 dates with the same week_seq, then find inventory rows on those dates for the same item. That's exactly the same as original: cs join inventory on item, then join d2 on inv_date_sk, and filter d1.week_seq = d2.week_seq. The order of joins doesn't matter for inner joins. The condition `inv_quantity_on_hand < cs_quantity` is applied in the join, equivalent to WHERE. So this is equivalent.

Does this query help performance? The CTE `filtered_cs` computes the same as the inner part of original. Then it joins to d2 and inventory. The planner might choose to inline the CTE and produce the same plan as original. However, if we add `MATERIALIZED`, it will materialize the filtered_cs CTE, which is very small (estimated 9 rows). This could prevent the planner from reordering the joins in a bad way? The original plan already computes the filtered cs side first (cost 128k) and then joins d2 and inventory. So materializing it might not change the plan, but it could reduce the cost if the filtered_cs is computed once and reused? But it's only used once. So no benefit. The main cost is still the `filtered_cs` computation. The CTE doesn't help with that. To improve that, we need an index.

What if we push the inventory condition into the CTE using EXISTS? That would reduce the filtered_cs to only rows that have a matching inventory, which could be smaller. But the original plan already joins inventory after, and the final result is 1 row, so the filtered_cs before inventory is already small. The cost is in computing filtered_cs, not in joining inventory. So EXISTS in CTE might not reduce the cost of computing filtered_cs because the expensive part is scanning catalog_sales for year 2000 and wholesale cost. The EXISTS would add more work. So not helpful.

Thus, the SQL rewrite is essentially cosmetic. The real optimization is index creation. Let's structure the final answer:
- Brief analysis of bottlenecks: The main cost is the nested loop join of `catalog_sales` with `date_dim d1` for year 2000, with `cs_wholesale_cost` applied after heap fetch. The unused left joins were already removed. The inventory join is efficient with primary key. The final aggregates are on a small result set.
- Optimization strategies with scores:
  1. Remove unused left joins (already done) - score 1 (minor).
  2. Use explicit aliases and INNER JOINs (already done) - score 1.
  3. Replace redundant MIN(i_item_sk) and MIN(inv_item_sk) with MIN(cs_item_sk) - score 1 (minor, equivalence due to joins).
  4. Create composite index on `catalog_sales(cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost)` - score 9 (most efficient). This allows index-only filtering of wholesale cost during the date range scan.
  5. Consider index on `inventory(inv_date_sk, inv_item_sk, inv_quantity_on_hand)` to avoid heap fetches for quantity filter - score 5.
- Final SQL: Provide the cleaned query with redundant MIN replaced.
- Provide DDL for indexes.
- Conclude with TERMINATE.

Let's write the final SQL carefully. Original pre_rewrite_sql:
SELECT MIN(i.i_item_sk),
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name),
       MIN(d1.d_week_seq),
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk),
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number),
       MIN(inv.inv_item_sk)
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
WHERE d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq
  AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2000
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'S'
  AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 6 AND 8
  AND i.i_category IN ('Children', 'Men', 'Women')
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 69 AND 89;

We can replace MIN(i.i_item_sk) with MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) and MIN(inv.inv_item_sk) with MIN(cs.cs_item_sk). Also, we can remove the item join entirely if we use EXISTS, but let's keep it simple. The final SQL:
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_i_item_sk,
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_w_warehouse_name,
       MIN(d1.d_week_seq) AS min_d1_d_week_seq,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_cs_item_sk,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_cs_order_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_inv_item_sk
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
WHERE d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq
  AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2000
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'S'
  AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 6 AND 8
  AND i.i_category IN ('Children', 'Men', 'Women')
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 69 AND 89;

This is equivalent. We can also remove the `i` join by using `EXISTS`:
  AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM item i WHERE i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk AND i.i_category IN (...))
But then we can't remove the join easily because we need to ensure only one row? Actually if we use EXISTS, we can remove the `JOIN item i`. The query would be:
SELECT ...
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv ...
...
WHERE ...
  AND EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM item i WHERE i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk AND i.i_category IN (...))
...
This might be slightly more efficient because it's a semi-join. The planner might still do an index lookup. It could also use a hash semi-join. Let's include this as it's a valid rewrite. However, we must ensure equivalence: The original INNER JOIN to item would duplicate rows if item had multiple rows for same i_item_sk. But `i_item_sk` is the primary key, so no duplicates. EXISTS is equivalent. So we can remove the item join and use EXISTS. This makes the query more efficient because it avoids joining to item for each row? Actually the planner will do a semi-join, which is essentially the same. But it's cleaner.

Similarly, we could use EXISTS for customer_demographics and household_demographics? They are joins on primary keys, so no duplicates. But they are small and used for filters. Could use EXISTS, but not necessary.

Let's write the final SQL with EXISTS for item:

SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_i_item_sk,
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_w_warehouse_name,
       MIN(d1.d_week_seq) AS min_d1_d_week_seq,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_cs_item_sk,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_cs_order_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_inv_item_sk
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
WHERE d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq
  AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2000
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'S'
  AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 6 AND 8
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 69 AND 89
  AND EXISTS (
      SELECT 1
      FROM item i
      WHERE i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
        AND i.i_category IN ('Children', 'Men', 'Women')
  );

This is equivalent and might allow the planner to use a semi-join. I'll use this as the final rewritten SQL.

Now, index recommendations:
1. `CREATE INDEX idx_catalog_sales_sold_date_wholesale ON catalog_sales (cs_sold_date_sk, cs_wholesale_cost);` -- high impact.
2. `CREATE INDEX idx_inventory_date_item_quantity ON inventory (inv_date_sk, inv_item_sk, inv_quantity_on_hand);` -- medium impact, helps filter quantity in index. Note: inventory_pkey already covers (inv_date_sk, inv_item_sk), adding quantity as included column might help. In PostgreSQL, we can use `INCLUDE`:
`CREATE INDEX idx_inventory_date_item_quantity ON inventory (inv_date_sk, inv_item_sk) INCLUDE (inv_quantity_on_hand);`
But since the condition is `inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity`, the quantity is compared to an outer value. Having it in the index allows index-only scan to evaluate the filter without heap fetch. However, the index scan already uses primary key, and the filter is applied after fetching the row. Including quantity in the index could avoid heap fetch for non-matching rows. But since only ~2 rows per probe, heap fetch is not a big cost. Still, it's a good suggestion.

3. For customer_demographics, maybe index on (cd_demo_sk, cd_marital_status, cd_dep_count) but it's already primary key. The filter is on cd_marital_status and cd_dep_count, but it's applied after index lookup on cd_demo_sk. Since there are few rows, not a bottleneck.

4. For household_demographics, filter on hd_buy_potential. It's small (7200 rows), seq scan is fine.

We'll present these.

Now, about the previous report: It said the enhanced SQL is semantically equivalent but no cost change. Our analysis agrees that SQL rewrite alone won't change cost. The real fix is indexing. So we can state that the query is already structurally optimized (after removing unused joins and aliases). We assign rewrite score for index strategy as 9/10. We'll provide the final SQL and DDL. Then conclude with TERMINATE? Since we are providing a rewritten SQL (with EXISTS and redundant MIN replacement), we did rewrite it. So maybe we don't need TERMINATE. The instruction says include TERMINATE if no further optimization is needed. But we are providing optimization (SQL rewrite + indexes). So I won't include TERMINATE. Wait, the user says: "If you find the SQL query is already well-optimized or too simple to require any changes, you can conclude that it doesn't need further optimization and then include the word 'TERMINATE' at the end of your response." We found changes are needed (indexes). So no TERMINATE. But the SQL rewrite itself is minor. I think it's safe to not include TERMINATE. However, the previous report already terminated? No, it said terminate false. So we should not terminate. We'll provide the final answer.

Statement 1 (id=1) preserves the original query semantics exactly, keeping all joins including `item`, `promotion`... wait, it drops the left outer joins to `promotion` and `catalog_returns`. Both enhanced statements drop these LEFT JOINs, which is valid because the LEFT JOIN to `promotion` cannot add or filter rows (projection only references other tables), and the LEFT JOIN to `catalog_returns` also cannot filter. So both are semantically equivalent.

Comparing id=0 vs id=1: id=0 converts the `item` join into an EXISTS semi-join, which is fine since MIN(i_item_sk) = MIN(cs_item_sk) under the join equality. However, id=0 replaces `MIN(i_item_sk)` with `MIN(cs.cs_item_sk)` — equivalent. id=1 keeps the original join structure but reorders predicates and joins for better pushdown, uses table aliases consistently, and adds column aliases for readability. Both apply similar optimizations (dropping provably-null LEFT JOINs). id=1 is more conservative and readable, keeps a simple join plan that the optimizer can efficiently handle (fact table joined to dimension keys with local filters pushed early: d_year, wholesale cost, category, demographics), whereas id=0's EXISTS rewrite gives no real benefit over a PK join. id=1's predicate ordering also aligns filters to their tables cleanly, aiding predicate pushdown. Overall id=1 is the most effective and safe rewrite.

Comparing the three EXPLAIN outputs node by node, the three plans are structurally and numerically almost identical. The original plan has a total cost of 131898.99..131899.00, while both the rewritten and enhanced plans come in at 131899.10..131899.11 — a nominal increase of roughly 0.11 cost units (about 0.0001%), which is statistically negligible. The most expensive plan node in all three plans is the innermost Nested Loop feeding the Parallel Hash Join (cost starting at ~128560), and this node is unchanged across all variants. The rewritten plan differs from the original only in that a join condition (i_item_sk = inv_item_sk) that was previously implicit in the inventory index scan is now expressed as an explicit Join Filter on the parent Nested Loop, and table aliases were introduced; this reshuffling slightly reattributed cost (e.g., the middle Nested Loop moved from 130889.93 to 130890.03) but did not change the plan shape. The enhanced plan is byte-for-byte identical to the rewritten plan, indicating that the optimization suggestions produced no further measurable effect on the planner's chosen path. Scan strategies (Parallel Index Scan on date_dim, Index Scans on catalog_sales, customer_demographics, item, inventory, warehouse, date_dim d3, and Seq Scans on household_demographics and date_dim d2), join algorithms (Nested Loops, Hash Join, Parallel Hash Join), parallelism (1 worker planned), row estimates, and memory structures (the small Hash for household_demographics and the Parallel Hash for the d1/d2 join) are all preserved across the three plans. There is no evidence of sort elimination, scan type transitions, or join algorithm changes, because the original plan already employed an index-driven, parallel access path with no explicit Sort nodes. In short, the rewrite and enhancement were cosmetic/structural in nature with no cost or resource impact.

1. Cost Efficiency:
- Overall cost change: Total plan cost increased marginally from 131898.99 (original) to 131899.10 (rewritten and enhanced), an increase of approximately 0.0001%. This is effectively a neutral change; the rewrite did not introduce overhead of any practical significance.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: The dominant node in all three plans is the deep Nested Loop chain rooted at the catalog_sales/date_dim access path (startup cost ≈ 128560). Its cost is identical in all three plans, confirming that the query's cost center — the filtering of catalog_sales via the sold-date index combined with dimension lookups — was untouched by the rewrite.

2. Plan Characteristics:

Let's write the in these final answer. pairs.

Consider the set of qualifying catalog_sales rows (after all non-inventory filters). For each (item, week), let `max_cs_qty` be the maximum `cs_quantity` among qualifying catalog_sales rows for that item and week. Then an inventory row for that (item, week) with quantity Q is part of some pair iff Q < max_cs_qty. Because if Q < max_cs_qty, then there is some cs row with cs_quantity = max_cs_qty > Q, so the pair exists. If Q >= max_cs_qty, then for all cs rows, cs_quantity <= max_cs_qty <= Q, so Q < cs_quantity is false. So the set of inventory rows that appear in the join is exactly those with `inv_quantity_on_hand < max_cs_qty` for their (item, week). Therefore, `min_warehouse_name` over the join is the minimum warehouse name among inventory rows that satisfy `inv_quantity_on_hand < max_cs_qty` for their (item, week), where `max_cs_qty` is derived from qualifying catalog_sales rows. This is a huge insight! It means we can compute the min warehouse name without joining inventory to every catalog_sales row. We can:
1. Compute `max_cs_qty` per (item, week) from qualifying catalog_sales rows.
2. Join that to inventory on (item, week) and filter `inv_quantity_on_hand < max_cs_qty`.
3. Join to warehouse and take MIN(w_warehouse_name).

This is much more efficient because step 1 aggregates catalog_sales to (item, week) and takes MAX(cs_quantity). Step 2 joins inventory to this aggregated set, which is much smaller than the original join because it's per (item, week) and we only need to find the min warehouse name. We can even use `DISTINCT ON` or `MIN` with a join to warehouse. Since we only need the overall min warehouse name, we can do:
```sql
SELECT MIN(w.w_warehouse_name)
FROM (
  SELECT cs.cs_item_sk, d1.d_week_seq, MAX(cs.cs_quantity) AS max_cs_qty
  FROM catalog_sales cs
  JOIN item i ON ...
  JOIN customer_demographics cd ON ...
  JOIN household_demographics hd ON ...
  JOIN date_dim d1 ON ...
  JOIN date_dim d3 ON ...
  WHERE ... non-inventory filters ...
  GROUP BY cs.cs_item_sk, d1.d_week_seq
) cs_agg
JOIN inventory inv ON inv.inv_item_sk = cs_agg.cs_item_sk
JOIN date_dim d2 ON d2.d_date_sk = inv.inv_date_sk AND d2.d_week_seq = cs_agg.d_week_seq
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
WHERE inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs_agg.max_cs_qty;
```
This is equivalent to the original min_warehouse_name. And for the other aggregates, we can use the same `cs_agg`? Wait, the other aggregates are MIN over the joined rows. For `MIN(cs_item_sk)`, it's just the min item_sk among qualifying catalog_sales rows that have at least one matching inventory row. A qualifying catalog_sales row must have at least one inventory row with quantity < cs_quantity. This is equivalent to: for its (item, week), the minimum inventory quantity is < cs_quantity. But if we use `max_cs_qty` per (item, week), we can't determine if a specific cs row has a match; we only know the max. However, for the overall MIN over all joined rows, we can use the fact that the joined rows are exactly the inventory rows with quantity < max_cs_qty for their (item, week). But the MIN of `cs_item_sk` over joined rows is the minimum `cs_item_sk` among pairs. This is the minimum item_sk that has at least one cs row with cs_quantity > some inventory quantity in the same week. This is not

- From qualifying catalog_sales rows: `max_cs_qty` per (item, week).

This is a substantial rewrite. Let's check correctness:
- `filtered_cs` gets all catalog_sales rows satisfying all non-inventory filters. Note: The original query also had `d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq` and `inv_quantity_on_hand < cs_quantity`. We removed those from `filtered_cs` and handle via `inv_week_min` and `cs_agg`.
- `inv_week_min` computes the min inventory quantity per item and week. This is derived from inventory joined to date_dim on date_sk. It groups by item and week. This is correct for checking existence: a cs row will have a match iff `cs_quantity > min_inv_qty` for its item and week. Because if the minimum inventory quantity is less than cs_quantity, then there exists an inventory row with quantity <= min < cs_quantity? Wait, if min_inv_qty is the minimum, then min_inv_qty < cs_quantity implies there exists an inventory row with quantity = min_inv_qty < cs_quantity. If min_inv_qty >= cs_quantity, then all inventory rows have quantity >= min_inv_qty >= cs_quantity, so none are < cs_quantity. So the condition `f.cs_quantity > iwm.min_inv_qty` is exactly correct.
- `qualifying_cs` selects the cs rows that have at least one matching inventory row. This is correct.
- The final SELECT computes the MINs over `qualifying_cs` for item, week, order. Since `cs_item_sk` = `inv_item_sk` for all joined rows, we use `cs_item_sk` for all three. This is correct.
- `cs_agg` computes the max cs_quantity per item and week from `filtered_cs`. This is correct for determining which inventory rows contribute to the join. Note: `filtered_cs` contains all cs rows satisfying non-inventory filters, even those that might not have a match. But for a given (item, week), if `filtered_cs` has rows, the max cs_quantity is the maximum among them. The set of inventory rows that have quantity < some cs_quantity in `filtered_cs` is exactly those with quantity < max_cs_qty. So `cs_agg` correctly captures the necessary max. It doesn't matter if some cs rows don't have a match; the max is what determines the upper bound for inventory quantity that can match. If the max cs_quantity is, say, 10, then any inventory row with quantity < 10 will match the cs row that has quantity 10. So it will be included. If the max cs_quantity row itself doesn't have a match? Wait, if max_cs_qty is from a cs row, then by definition there is a cs row with that quantity. For an inventory row with quantity < max_cs_qty, it will match that specific cs row (since same item, week, and quantity < max_cs_qty). So it will be in the join. Therefore, the set of inventory rows that appear in the join is exactly those with quantity < max_cs_qty. This holds regardless of other cs rows. So `cs_agg` is correct.
- The subquery for `min_warehouse_name` joins `cs_agg` to inventory on item, then to date_dim d2 on date_sk and week_seq = cs_agg.d_week_seq, then to warehouse. It filters `inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < c.max_cs_qty`. This gives all inventory rows that contribute to the join, and takes MIN(w_warehouse_name). This is correct.

Potential issues:
- `inv_week_min` groups by `inv.inv_item_sk` and `d2.d_week_seq`. This could be a large CTE. But it only needs to be computed once. The optimizer might materialize it. It has 133M inventory rows, grouping by item and week. As estimated earlier, the number of groups could be ~1.8M. This is much smaller than 133M. So it should be efficient.
- `filtered_cs` might be large, but it's filtered by d1.d_year=2000 and other conditions. The original plan estimated 1 row after all joins, but `filtered_cs` before inventory might have more rows. The original plan estimated 1788 rows after d1, catalog_sales, household, customer, item joins. So `filtered_cs` might have ~1788 rows. That's very small. So `cs_agg` will be tiny.
- `qualifying_cs` joins `filtered_cs` to `inv_week_min` on (item, week) and filters `cs_quantity > min_inv_qty`. Since `filtered_cs` is small (~1788 rows), this join is fast if `inv_week_min` has an index on (inv_item_sk, d_week_seq). But CTEs in PostgreSQL don't automatically get indexes unless materialized and then scanned. If `inv_week_min` is materialized, it may not have an index. The join would be a hash join or nested loop with seq scan on `inv_week_min`. Since `filtered_cs` is small, a hash join with `inv_week_min` (1.8M rows) might be used. But we can improve by creating an index? We can't. However, we can avoid materializing `inv_week_min` and instead use a correlated subquery? Or we can rewrite to use `EXISTS` with inventory directly, which might use the inventory index. Let's compare:
Original plan with inventory join: cost 131899. Our rewrite's main cost will be:
- Scan inventory 133M rows to compute `inv_week_min`. That's a seq scan on 133M rows. The original plan did not scan all inventory; it used index lookups. A full scan of 133M rows could be very expensive. The cost of scanning 133M rows is significant. The original plan cost 131899 is in arbitrary units. A seq scan of 133M rows in PostgreSQL might cost around 133M * (1 + random_page_cost?) Actually sequential scan cost per page is 1.0, plus CPU. 133M rows would be many pages. This could be much more expensive than the original nested loop which only did a few thousand index lookups. So pre-aggregating the entire inventory table might be a bad idea if the query is selective. The original query only looks up inventory for the few thousand candidate catalog_sales rows. Scanning all of inventory to build `inv_week_min` would be a full table scan, which is likely much slower. So this approach is only good if the query needs to process a large fraction of inventory. Here, the filters on catalog_sales are very selective (estimated 1 row final), so scanning 133M inventory rows is a huge waste. Therefore, the original nested loop approach is actually better for selective queries. Our rewrite would be worse due to full inventory scan.

What about using the `EXISTS` approach without pre-aggregating inventory? That was the earlier idea:
```sql
SELECT

...

This still uses index lookups on inventory for each candidate cs row, but avoids fan-out. It also does a separate subquery for min_warehouse_name. The `EXISTS` can stop at first match, which might be faster than joining all matches. The subquery for min_warehouse_name can also use EXISTS and order by warehouse name, checking up to 10 warehouses. This might be more efficient than the original because it avoids materializing all inventory matches for the aggregation. Let's analyze the cost:
- The outer query scans catalog_sales (filtered) and for each row does an EXISTS on inventory. This is similar to the original join, but the original join also joins warehouse and date_dim d2 and multiplies rows. The original plan already does a nested loop to inventory and then to warehouse. The EXISTS would do the same but stop at first match. Since we only need to know if there is at least one match, and the original needs all matches to compute MIN(w_warehouse_name) and MIN(inv_item_sk) (though inv_item_sk is redundant), the original has to process all matching inventory rows. If there are many inventory rows per (item, week), the EXISTS could be much faster. But in TPCDS, for a given item and week, there are up to 7 days * 10 warehouses = 70 inventory rows. That's not huge. The original processes all 70 and takes MIN. The EXISTS stops at the first. This could reduce work by a factor of up to 70. However, the original also needs the warehouse name for the MIN. To compute MIN(w_warehouse_name), we need to consider all matching inventory rows, not just the first. So we cannot use EXISTS for the warehouse min; we need to process all matching inventory rows to find the minimum warehouse name. But we can do that in a separate subquery that also scans catalog_sales. That would double the work on catalog_sales. Since catalog_sales is the large table (14M) but filtered to a small set, doubling the scan of the filtered set might be okay if the filtered set is small. But if the filtered set is small, the original join's inventory fan-out is also small. So the gain might be limited.

Given the original plan's estimated final rows is 1, the filtered catalog_sales set is very small. The original query likely executes very fast. The actual execution might be fast if the filters are selective. The cost model might be pessimistic. The previous report says the enhanced plan cost is marginally higher, but execution time might be the same. So the original is likely already optimal.

```sql
SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_i_item_sk,
       MIN(w.w_warehouse_name) AS min_w_warehouse_name,
       MIN(d1.d_week_seq) AS min_d1_week_seq,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_cs_item_sk,
       MIN(cs.cs_order_number) AS min_cs_order_number,
       MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_inv_item_sk
FROM catalog_sales cs
JOIN inventory inv ON cs.cs_item_sk = inv.inv_item_sk
JOIN warehouse w ON w.w_warehouse_sk = inv.inv_warehouse_sk
JOIN item i ON i.i_item_sk = cs.cs_item_sk
JOIN customer_demographics cd ON cs.cs_bill_cdemo_sk = cd.cd_demo_sk
JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk
JOIN date_dim d1 ON cs.cs_sold_date_sk = d1.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d2 ON inv.inv_date_sk = d2.d_date_sk
JOIN date_dim d3 ON cs.cs_ship_date_sk = d3.d_date_sk
WHERE d1.d_week_seq = d2.d_week_seq
  AND inv.inv_quantity_on_hand < cs.cs_quantity
  AND d3.d_date > d1.d_date + INTERVAL '3 day'
  AND hd.hd_buy_potential = '1001-5000'
  AND d1.d_year = 2000
  AND cd.cd_marital_status = 'S'
  AND cd.cd_dep_count BETWEEN 6 AND 8
  AND i.i_category IN ('Children', 'Men', 'Women')
  AND cs.cs_wholesale_cost BETWEEN 69 AND 89;

SELECT MIN(cs.cs_item_sk) AS min_i_item_sk,

FROM catalog_sales cs

JOIN household_demographics hd ON cs.cs_bill_hdemo_sk = hd.hd_demo_sk

</analysis>
The rewritten SQL makes three key changes:
1. LEFT OUTER JOINs to promotion and catalog_returns are removed. Neither table contributes columns to SELECT or WHERE, and left joins never eliminate rows, so removing them does not change the result.
2. The join to date_dim d3 is replaced by the direct predicate cs_ship_date_sk > cs_sold_date_sk + 3. In TPC-DS, d_date_sk is a sequential integer encoding of d_date, so d3.d_date > d1.d_date + interval '3 day' is exactly cs_ship_date_sk > cs_sold_date_sk + 3 (NULL ship dates are excluded in both formulations).
3. Redundant MIN aggregates are collapsed: the equality joins i_item_sk = cs_item_sk and inv_item_sk = cs_item_sk guarantee MIN(i_item_sk) = MIN(inv_item_sk) = MIN(cs_item_sk), so the duplicates carry identical values.

All filter predicates (d_year, category, marital status, dep count, buy potential, wholesale cost) and join conditions are preserved. Aliases and predicate placement are cosmetic. The result rows and values are identical.

Comparing the original EXPLAIN output with the rewritten and enhanced versions, the three plans are structurally identical: the same join tree, the same scan methods, the same join algorithms, and the same parallelism are preserved throughout. The only measurable differences are (1) a marginal reduction in the total estimated cost (from 131899.00 to 131898.98) and (2) a reduction in the estimated average row width at several upper nodes (e.g., Gather width drops from 52 to 44, Partial Aggregate's input width from 78 to 70, and the intermediate Nested Loop widths shrink accordingly). These width reductions indicate a tighter projection of columns flowing up the plan — the rewritten query avoids carrying redundant columns through the upper join levels — but the optimizer clearly considers the original plan already near-optimal for this workload. No scan-type transitions, join-algorithm changes, sort eliminations, or worker adjustments occur; the plan already uses parallel execution with one worker, parallel index/sequential scans, index nested loops, and a parallel hash join. Therefore, the "improvement" is cosmetic in cost terms (≈0.000015% reduction) rather than structural.

1. Cost Efficiency
- Overall cost change: The total estimated cost decreased only marginally, from 131,899.00 in the original plan to 131,898.98 in both the rewritten and enhanced plans — a reduction of approximately 0.000015%. This is effectively negligible and indicates the original plan was already highly optimized by the planner.
- Cost change at the most expensive plan node: The dominant cost node, the Parallel Hash Join feeding the deeply nested join chain (cost ≈ 128,560.16..130,575.08), remains unchanged across all three plans. Likewise, the innermost expensive component — the Nested Loop joining catalog_sales against date_dim d1 (cost ≈ 126,945.71) — is identical in all versions. No cost relief was achieved at the hot spots.

2. Plan Characteristics
- Scan type transitions: None. All three plans use the same access paths: Parallel Index Scan on date_dim d1 via idx_date_dim_sk, Index Scans on catalog_sales (idx_catalog_sales_sold_date), customer_demographics, item, inventory (pkey), warehouse (pkey), and date_dim d3, plus a Parallel Seq Scan on date_dim d2 and a Seq Scan on household_demographics. No Seq Scan was converted to an Index Scan or vice versa.
- Join algorithm refinements: None. The join strategy is unchanged: a Parallel Hash Join on (d_week_seq) at the top of the inner subtree, with Nested Loop joins (index-driven) for all other relationships, including the date-interval join filter (d3.d_date > d1.d_date + 3 days).
- Elimination of explicit sorting and intermediate result sets: No explicit Sort nodes exist in any version, and none needed to be removed — the plan already relies on index ordering and hash aggregation. The only change in intermediate result shape is a narrower tuple width flowing upward (e.g., 78→70 and 86→78 at the nested loop levels, 52→44 at the Gather node), which slightly reduces the data volume carried through the upper plan levels.

3. Resource Utilization
- Memory usage: The Hash node over household_demographics (≈1,200 rows) and the Parallel Hash node are identical in all plans; memory consumption for hash tables is unchanged. The modest tuple-width reductions described above would yield a marginal (practically negligible) decrease in per-tuple memory during aggregation and gather, but no Hash or Buffer behavior changed.
- Worker count adjustment: Unchanged. All plans use parallel query with Workers Planned: 1 (i.e., 2 parallel participants including the leader) at the Gather node, and both the Parallel Seq Scan and Parallel Index Scan on date_dim retain their parallel design.

4. Other Improvements
- The primary benefit of the rewrite is improved column pruning/projection: the rewritten and enhanced queries alias tables (cs, hd, cd, i, inv, w, d1–d3) and evidently project fewer columns upward, reducing estimated row widths by 8 bytes at multiple nodes (Gather 52→44; Partial Aggregate input 78→70). This trims serialization and tuple-sloting overhead slightly without affecting the plan's shape or cost model materially.
- The enhanced plan is byte-for-byte equivalent in structure and cost to the rewritten plan, confirming that no additional optimization opportunity (e.g., join reordering, predicate pushdown, index selection, or parallelism tuning) was available beyond what the original planner already produced.
- Conclusion: The original execution plan was already well-optimized; the rewrite delivers only a negligible cost reduction and slimmer tuples. For meaningful further gains, attention should shift to the dominant cost drivers — the catalog_sales index scan volume (~1,788 estimated rows before demographic filtering) and the large nested-loop join chain — e.g., via statistics tuning, partial indexes on (cs_wholesale_cost, cs_sold_date_sk), or materialized pre-aggregation.
